<a href="#part0002.html_notes" class="calibre2"><span
class="bold">Notes</span></a>

The pagination of this electronic edition does not match the edition
from which it was created. To locate a specific entry, please use your
e-book reader’s search tools.

Below you will find the source material for the stories we’ve told in
this book. We are grateful, and indebted, to the many scholars, writers,
and others whose research we relied upon. Let’s also raise a glass to
Wikipedia. It has improved immeasurably over the years that we have been
writing books; it is extraordinarily valuable as a first stop to
discover primary sources on nearly any topic. Thanks to all those who
have contributed to it intellectually, financially, or otherwise.

<span class="bold1">CHAPTER 1: WHAT DOES IT MEAN TO THINK LIKE A
FREAK?</span>

  1 <span class="bold4">“IS A COLLEGE DEGREE STILL ‘WORTH IT’ ”?</span>:
See Stephen J. Dubner, “Freakonomics Goes to College, Parts 1 and 2,”
Freakonomics Radio, July 30, 2012, and August 16, 2012. As for the value
of college and the returns on investment, the economist David Card has
written widely and well on this topic. See also Ronald G. Ehrenberg,
“American Higher Education in Transition,” <span class="ital">Journal of
Economic Perspectives</span> 26, no. 1 (Winter 2012). / 1
“<span class="bold1">Is it a good idea to pass along a family business
. . . ?”:</span> See Stephen J. Dubner, “The Church of Scionology,”
Freakonomics Radio, August 3, 2011. Some of the relevant papers are:
Marianne Bertrand and Antoinette Schoar, “The Role of Family in Family
Firms,” <span class="ital">Journal of Economic Perspectives</span> 20,
no. 2 (Spring 2006); Vikas Mehrotra, Randall Morck, Jungwook Shim, and
Yupana Wiwattanakantang, “Adoptive Expectations: Rising Sons in Japanese
Family Firms,” <span class="ital">Journal of Financial Economics</span>
108, no. 3 (June 2013); and Francisco Pérez-González, “Inherited Control
and Firm Performance,” <span class="ital">American Economic
Review</span> 96, no. 5 (2006) / 1 “<span class="bold1">Whatever
happened to the carpal tunnel syndrome epidemic?”:</span> See Stephen J.
Dubner, “Whatever Happened to the Carpal Tunnel Epidemic?,” Freakonomics
Radio, September 12, 2013. Drawn from research by Bradley Evanoff, an
M.D. who studies occupational medicine at Washington University; among
his relevant papers: T. Armstrong, A. M. Dale, A. Franzblau, and
Evanoff, “Risk Factors for Carpal Tunnel Syndrome and Median Neuropathy
in a Working Population,” <span class="ital">Journal of Occupational and
Environmental Medicine</span> 50, no. 12 (December 2008).

  3 <span class="bold4">IMAGINE YOU ARE A SOCCER PLAYER</span>: The
statistics in this section were drawn from: Pierre-André Chiappori,
Steven D. Levitt, Timothy Groseclose, “Testing Mixed-Strategy Equilibria
When Players Are Heterogeneous: The Case of Penalty Kicks in Soccer,”
<span class="ital">The American Economic Review</span> 92, no. 4
(September 2002); see also Stephen J. Dubner and Steven D. Levitt, “How
to Take Penalties: Freakonomics Explains,” <span class="ital">The (U.K.)
Times</span>, June 12, 2010. For the speed of soccer ball, see
Eleftherios Kellis and Athanasios Katis, “Biomechanical Characteristics
and Determinants of Instep Soccer Kick,” <span class="ital">Journal of
Sports Science and Medicine</span> 6 (2007). Thanks to Solomon Dubner
for his insights into this passage, and for his great interest in footy.

  9 <span class="bold4">“IF YOU’RE GRUMPY, WHO THE HELL WANTS TO MARRY
YOU?”</span>: Spoken by the irrepressible and inimitable Justin Wolfers
in Stephen J. Dubner, “Why Marry, Part 1,” Freakonomics Radio, February
13, 2014. See: Betsey Stevenson and Wolfers, “Marriage and Divorce:
Changes and Their Driving Forces,” NBER working paper 12944 (March
2007); Alois Stutzer and Bruno S. Frey, “Does Marriage Make People
Happy, or Do Happy People Get Married?,” IZA discussion paper (October
2005).

10 <span class="bold4">EVEN THE SMARTEST PEOPLE TEND TO SEEK OUT
INFORMATION THAT CONFIRMS WHAT THEY ALREADY THINK</span>: See Stephen J.
Dubner, “The Truth Is Out There . . . Isn’t It?,” Freakonomics Radio,
November 23, 2011; drawn from research conducted by, among others, the
Cultural Cognition Project. / 10 <span class="bold1">It’s also tempting
to run with a herd:</span> See Stephen J. Dubner, “Riding the Herd
Mentality,” Freakonomics Radio, June 21, 2012.

11 <span class="bold4">“FEW PEOPLE THINK MORE THAN TWO OR THREE TIMES A
YEAR”</span>: Like many historical quotes, this one is hard to verify
for certain, but Shaw was at least famous during his lifetime for having
said this. In 1933, <span class="ital">Reader’s Digest</span> attributed
the quote to Shaw, as did many other publications. Hat tip to Garson
O’Toole of QuoteInvestigator.com, who provided considerable help in
tracing this quote.

11 <span class="bold4">CHILD CAR SEATS ARE A WASTE OF TIME</span>: See
Joseph J. Doyle Jr. and Steven D. Levitt, “Evaluating the Effectiveness
of Child Safety Seats and Seat Belts in Protecting Children From
Injury,” <span class="ital">Economic Inquiry</span> 48, no. 3 (July
2010); Stephen J. Dubner and Levitt, “The Seat-Belt Solution,”
<span class="ital">The New York Times Magazine,</span> July 10, 2005;
Levitt and Dubner, <span class="ital">SuperFreakonomics</span> (William
Morrow, 2009). / 11 <span class="bold1">The local-food movement can
actually hurt the environment:</span> See Christopher L. Weber and H.
Scott Matthews, “Food-Miles and the Relative Climate Impacts of Food
Choices in the United States,” <span class="ital">Environmental Science
& Technology</span> 42, no. 10 (April 2008); and Stephen J. Dubner, “You
Eat What You Are, Part 2,” Freakonomics Radio, June 7, 2012.

11 <span class="bold4">OUR DISASTROUS MEETING WITH DAVID CAMERON</span>:
Thanks to Rohan Silva for the invitation to this and subsequent meetings
(though never again with Mr. Cameron himself!) and to David Halpern and
his Behavioral Insights Team. / 14 <span class="bold1">“The closest
thing the English have to a religion”:</span> See Nigel Lawson,
<span class="ital">The View from No. 11: Memoirs of a Tory
Radical</span> (Bantam Press, 1992) / 14 <span class="bold1">U.K.
health-care costs:</span> See Adam Jurd, “Expenditure on Healthcare in
the UK, 1997–2010,” Office for National Statistics, May 2, 2012 / 14
<span class="bold1">David Cameron biographical details:</span> We are
especially indebted to Francis Elliott and James Hanning’s
<span class="ital">Cameron: Practically a Conservative</span> (Fourth
Estate, 2012), originally published as <span class="ital">Cameron: The
Rise of the New Conservative</span>, a thorough if somewhat tabloidy
biography. / 16 <span class="bold1">A massive share of the costs go to
the final months:</span> for an interesting discussion of end-of-life
medical care, see Ezekiel J. Emanuel, “Better, if Not Cheaper, Care,”
<span class="ital">New York Times,</span> January 4, 2013.

<span class="bold1">CHAPTER 2: THE THREE HARDEST WORDS IN THE ENGLISH
LANGUAGE</span>

19 <span class="bold4">A LITTLE GIRL NAMED MARY</span>: Special thanks
to Amanda Waterman, a developmental psychologist at the University of
Leeds. There is a small but interesting literature on the topic of
unanswerable questions, among both children and adults, to which
Waterman is an important contributor. See Waterman and Mark Blades,
“Helping Children Correctly Say ‘I Don’t Know’ to Unanswerable
Questions,” <span class="ital">Journal of Experimental Psychology:
Applied</span> 17, no. 4 (2011); Waterman, Blades, and Christopher
Spencer, “Interviewing Children and Adults: The Effect of Question
Format on the Tendency to Speculate,” <span class="ital">Applied
Cognitive Psychology</span> 15 (2001); Waterman and Blades, “The Effect
of Delay and Individual Differences on Children’s Tendency to Guess,”
<span class="ital">Developmental Psychology</span> 49, no. 2 (February
2013); Alan Scoboria, Giuliana Mazzoni, and Irving Kirsch, “ ‘Don’t
Know’ Responding to Answerable and Unanswerable Questions During
Misleading and Hypnotic Interviews,” <span class="ital">Journal of
Experimental Psychology: Applied</span> 14, no. 3. (September 2008);
Claudia M. Roebers and Olivia Fernandez, “The Effects of Accuracy
Motivation and Children’s and Adults’ Event Recall, Suggestibility, and
Their Answers to Unanswerable Questions,” <span class="ital">Journal of
Cognition and Development</span> 3, no. 4 (2002).

20 <span class="bold4">“EVERYONE’S ENTITLED TO THEIR OWN OPINION BUT NOT
TO THEIR OWN FACTS”</span>: Moynihan said this at a Jerome Levy
Economics Institute Conference at the National Press Club in Washington,
D.C., on October 26, 1995. According to <span class="ital">The
Dictionary of Modern Proverbs</span> (Yale University Press, 2012) by
Charles Clay Doyle, Wolfgang Mieder, and Fred R. Shapiro, the phrase was
first said by Bernard M. Baruch.

21 <span class="bold4">BELIEF IN THE DEVIL AND “ENTREPRENEURS OF
ERROR”</span>: Thanks to Ed Glaeser for making this point in a lecture
given at an April 2006 conference at the University Chicago in honor of
Gary Becker. Devil poll numbers are from European Values Study 1990:
Integrated Dataset (EVS, 2011), GESIS Data Archive, Cologne. 9/11
numbers are from a Gallup poll: “Blame for Sept. 11 Attacks Unclear for
Many in Islamic World,” March 1, 2002; see also Matthew A. Gentzkow and
Jesse M. Shapiro, “Media, Education and Anti-Americanism in the Muslim
World,” <span class="ital">Journal of Economic Perspectives</span> 18,
no. 3 (Summer 2004).

23 <span class="bold4">THE FOLLY OF PREDICTION</span>: “Prediction is
very difficult . . .”: Niels Bohr was “fond of quoting” this line; it is
strongly associated with a fellow Dane, the prominent cartoonist Storm
P., although he is likely not the originator either. / 23
<span class="bold1">One of the most impressive studies:</span> See
Philip E. Tetlock, <span class="ital">Expert Political Judgment: How
Good Is It? How Can We Know?</span> (Princeton University Press, 2005);
and Stephen J. Dubner, “The Folly of Prediction,” Freakonomics Radio,
September 14, 2011. For economic predictions, see Jerker Denrell and
Christina Fang, “Predicting the Next Big Thing: Success as a Signal of
Poor Judgment,” <span class="ital">Management Science</span> 56, no. 10
(2010); for NFL predictions, see Christopher Avery and Judith Chevalier,
“Identifying Investor Sentiment From Price Paths: The Case of Football
Betting,” <span class="ital">Journal of Business</span> 72, no. 4
(1999). / 24 <span class="bold1">A similar study by a firm called CXO
Advisory Group:</span> See “Guru Grades,” CXO Advisory Group / 25
<span class="bold1">Smart people love to make smart-sounding
predictions:</span> See Paul Krugman, “Why Most Economists’ Predictions
Are Wrong,” <span class="ital">Red Herring</span>, June 1998. (Thanks to
the Internet Archive Wayback Machine.) / 26 <span class="bold1">More
than the GDP of all but eighteen countries:</span> market caps of
Google, Amazon, Facebook, and Apple are based on stock prices as of
February 11, 2014; the eighteen countries are: Australia, Brazil,
Canada, China, France, Germany, India, Indonesia, Italy, Japan, Mexico,
Russia, South Korea, Spain, the Netherlands, the U.K., the U.S., and
Turkey (see CIA World Factbook).

27 <span class="bold4">WE DON’T EVEN KNOW OURSELVES ALL THAT
WELL</span>: See Clayton R. Critcher and David Dunning, “How Chronic
Self-Views Influence (and Mislead) Self-Assessments of Task Performance:
Self-Views Shape Bottom-Up Experiences with the Task,”
<span class="ital">Journal of Personality and Social Psychology</span>
97, no. 6 (2009). (Thanks to Danny Kahneman and Tom Gilovich for leading
us to this paper.) See also: Dunning et al., “Why People Fail to
Recognize Their Own Incompetence,” <span class="ital">Current Directions
in Psychological Science</span> 12, no. 3 (June 2003).

27 <span class="bold4">WHEN ASKED TO RATE THEIR DRIVING SKILLS</span>:
See Iain A. McCormick, Frank H. Walkey, and Dianne E. Green,
“Comparative Perceptions of Driver Ability—A Confirmation and
Expansion,” <span class="ital">Accident Analysis & Prevention</span> 18,
no. 3 (June 1986); and Ola Svenson, “Are We All Less Risky and More
Skillful Than Our Fellow Drivers?,” <span class="ital">Acta
Psychologica</span> 47 (1981).

27 <span class="bold4">“ULTRACREPIDARIANISM”</span>: We are grateful to
the continuing research by Anders Ericsson and his many colleagues, much
of whose research is collected in Ericsson, Neil Charness, Paul J.
Feltovich, and Robert R. Hoffman, <span class="ital">The Cambridge
Handbook of Expertise and Expert Performance</span> (Cambridge
University Press, 2006); see also Steven D. Levitt, John A. List, and
Sally E. Sadoff, “Checkmate: Exploring Backward Induction Among Chess
Players,” <span class="ital">American Economics Review</span> 101, no. 2
(April 2011); Chris Argyris, “Teaching Smart People How to Learn,”
<span class="ital">Harvard Business Review,</span> May 1991. Our
definition of “ultracrepidarianism” is from FreeDictionary.com.

28 <span class="bold4">COSTS OF THE IRAQ WAR</span>: See Linda J.
Bilmes, “The Financial Legacy of Iraq and Afghanistan: How Wartime
Spending Decisions Will Constrain Future National Security Budgets,”
Harvard Kennedy School Faculty Research Working Paper Series RWP13-006
(March 2013); Amy Belasco, “The Cost of Iraq, Afghanistan, and Other
Global War on Terror Operations Since 9/11,” Congressional Research
Service, March 29, 2011.

30 <span class="bold4">AN ELDERLY CHRISTIAN RADIO PREACHER NAMED HAROLD
CAMPING</span>: See Robert D. McFadden, “Harold Camping, Dogged
Forecaster of the End of the World, Dies at 92,” <span class="ital">New
York Times</span>, December 17, 2013; Dan Amira, “A Conversation with
Harold Camping, Prophesier of Judgment Day,” Daily Intelligencer blog,
<span class="ital">New York Magazine</span>, May 11, 2011; Harold
Camping, “We Are Almost There!,” Familyradio.com. (Thanks to the
Internet Archive Wayback Machine.)

30 <span class="bold4">ROMANIAN WITCHES</span>: See Stephen J. Dubner,
“The Folly of Prediction,” Freakonomics Radio, September 14, 2011;
“Witches Threaten Romanian Taxman After New Labor Law,” BBC, January 6,
2011; Alison Mutler, “Romania’s Witches May Be Fined If Predictions
Don’t Come True,” Associated Press, February 8, 2011.

32 <span class="bold4">SHIP’S COMPASSES AND METAL INTERFERENCE</span>:
See A. R. T. Jonkers, <span class="ital">Earth’s Magnetism in the Age of
Sail</span> (Johns Hopkins University Press, 2003); T. A. Lyons,
<span class="ital">A Treatise on Electromagnetic Phenomena and on the
Compass and Its Deviations Aboard Ship, Vol. 2</span> (John Wiley &
Sons, 1903). Thanks to Jonathan Rosen for pointing out this idea.

32 <span class="bold4">CONSIDER A PROBLEM LIKE SUICIDE</span>: For a
fuller treatment of this topic, see Stephen J. Dubner, “The Suicide
Paradox,” Freakonomics Radio, August 31, 2011. We are particularly
indebted to the broad and deep research of David Lester, as well as
multiple interviews with him. We also relied heavily on David M. Cutler,
Edward L. Glaeser, and Karen E. Norberg, “Explaining the Rise in Youth
Suicide,” from Jonathan Gruber (editor), <span class="ital">Risky
Behavior Among Youths: An Economic Analysis</span> (University of
Chicago Press, 2001). Various reports from the Centers for Disease
Control and Prevention and the National Vital Statistics System were
very helpful; see also Robert E. McKeown, Steven P. Cuffe, and Richard
M. Schulz, “U.S. Suicide Rates by Age Group, 1970–2002: An Examination
of Recent Trends,” <span class="ital">American Journal of Public
Health</span> 96, no. 10 (October 2006). On the topic of the “suicide
paradox”—i.e., the link between suicide and increased well-being—see
Cutler et al. as well as: A. F. Henry and J. F. Short,
<span class="ital">Suicide and Homicide</span> (Free Press, 1954); David
Lester, “Suicide, Homicide, and the Quality of Life: An Archival Study,”
<span class="ital">Suicide and Life-Threatening Behavior</span>, 1693
(fall 1986); Lester, “Suicide, Homicide, and the Quality of Life in
Various Countries,” <span class="ital">Acta Psychiatrica
Scandinavica</span> 81 (1990); E. Hem et al., “Suicide Rates According
to Education with a Particular Focus on Physicians in Norway 1960–2000,”
<span class="ital">Psychological Medicine</span> 35, no. 6 (June 2005);
Mary C. Daly, Andrew J. Oswald, Daniel Wilson, Stephen Wu, “The
Happiness-Suicide Paradox,” Federal Reserve Bank of San Francisco
working paper 2010–30; Daly, Wilson, and Norman J. Johnson, “Relative
Status and Well-Being: Evidence from U.S. Suicide Deaths,” Federal
Reserve Bank of San Francisco working paper 2012–16. / 32
<span class="bold1">The U.S. homicide rate is lower than it’s been in
fifty years:</span> See James Alan Fox and Marianne W. Zawitz, “Homicide
Trends in the United States,” Bureau of Justice Statistics; and “Crime
in the United States 2012,” Federal Bureau of Investigation’s Uniform
Crime Reports, Table 16. / 32 <span class="bold1">The rate of traffic
fatalities is at a historic low:</span> See Stephen J. Dubner, “The Most
Dangerous Machine,” Freakonomics Radio, December 5, 2013; Ian Savage, a
Northwestern economist who studies transportation safety, was especially
helpful in compiling this research. See also: “Traffic Safety Facts:
2012 Motor Vehicle Crashes: Overview,” National Highway Traffic Safety
Administration, November 2013.

40 <span class="bold4">IN TRYING TO MEASURE THE KNOCK-ON EFFECTS OF
SENDING MILLIONS OF PEOPLE TO PRISON</span>: See Steven D. Levitt, “The
Effect of Prison Population Size on Crime Rates: Evidence from Prison
Overcrowding Litigation,” <span class="ital">The Quarterly Journal of
Economics</span> 111, no. 2 (May 1996) / 40 <span class="bold1">In
analyzing the relationship between abortion and crime . . . :</span> See
John J. Donohue III and Levitt, “The Impact of Legalized Abortion on
Crime,” <span class="ital">The Quarterly Journal of Economics</span>
116, no. 2 (May 2001).

41 <span class="bold4">A BETTER WAY TO GET GOOD FEEDBACK IS TO RUN A
FIELD EXPERIMENT</span>: One of the masters of modern field
experimentation is John List, with whom we’ve collaborated a good bit,
and whom we wrote about in Chapter 3 of
<span class="ital">SuperFreakonomics</span>. For an engaging tour of the
topic, see Uri Gneezy and John A. List, <span class="ital">The Why Axis:
Hidden Motives and the Undiscovered Economics of Everyday Life</span>
(Public Affairs, 2013).

42 <span class="bold4">DO EXPENSIVE WINES REALLY TASTE BETTER?</span>
For a fuller treatment of this topic, see Stephen J. Dubner, “Do More
Expensive Wines Taste Better?” Freakonomics Radio, December 16, 2010. It
includes the story of Steve Levitt’s blind taste test at the Society of
Fellows and Robin Goldstein’s extensive blind-taste experiments. For the
underlying research on Goldstein’s findings, see Goldstein, Johan
Almenberg, Anna Dreber, John W. Emerson, Alexis Herschkowitsch, and
Jacob Katz, “Do More Expensive Wines Taste Better? Evidence from a Large
Sample of Blind Tastings,” <span class="ital">Journal of Wine
Economics</span> 3, no. 1 (Spring 2008); see also Steven D. Levitt,
“Cheap Wine,” Freakonomics.com, July 16, 2008. While Goldstein’s
research suggests that wine experts are far more discerning than average
drinkers, there is further research that challenges even this
assumption. Another paper in <span class="ital">The Journal of Wine
Economics</span> found experts’ judgment to be—well, rather inexpert.
One study of judges at wine competitions, for instance, found that most
wines that win a gold medal in one competition received no award at all
in another. “Thus,” wrote the author, “many wines that are viewed as
extraordinarily good at some competitions are viewed as below average at
others.” See Robert T. Hodgson, “An Analysis of the Concordance Among 13
U.S. Wine Competitions,” <span class="ital">Journal of Wine
Economics</span> 4, no. 1 (spring 2009). / 45
<span class="bold1">Osteria L’Intrepido’s terrible wine list:</span>
Goldstein revealed his <span class="ital">Wine Spectator</span> Award of
Excellence prank at the American Association of Wine Economists’ 2008
annual conference. The incident received widespread media coverage.
<span class="ital">Wine Spectator</span> vigorously defended its award
system; the executive editor said the magazine never claimed to visit
every restaurant that applied for an award, and that it did its due
diligence on Osteria L’Intrepido—looking over its website and calling
the restaurant—but that it kept reaching an answering machine. See also:
Goldstein, “What Does It Take to Get a Wine Spectator Award of
Excellence,” Blindtaste.com, August 15, 2008.

47 <span class="bold4">REMEMBER THOSE BRITISH SCHOOLCHILDREN</span>: See
Amanda H. Waterman and Mark Blades, “Helping Children Correctly Say ‘I
Don’t Know’ to Unanswerable Questions, <span class="ital">Journal of
Experimental Psychology: Applied</span> 17, no. 4 (2011).

<span class="bold1">CHAPTER 3: WHAT’S YOUR PROBLEM?</span>

50 <span class="bold4">TEACHER SKILL</span>: See the two-part National
Bureau of Economic Research paper by Raj Chetty, John N. Friedman, and
Jonah E. Rockoff, “The Long-term Impacts of Teachers: Teach Value-added
and Student Outcomes in Adulthood” (September 2013) / 50
<span class="bold1">Smart women now have so many more job
options:</span> See Marigee P. Bacolod, “Do Alternative Opportunities
Matter? The Role of Female Labor Markets in the Decline of Teacher
Supply and Teacher Quality, 1940–1990,” <span class="ital">Review of
Economics and Statistics</span> 89, no. 4 (November 2007); and Harold O.
Levy, “Why the Best Don’t Teach,” <span class="ital">The New York
Times</span>, September 9, 2000. / 50 <span class="bold1">Finnish
teachers (e.g.) vs. American teachers:</span> See “Top Performing
Countries,” Center on International Education Benchmarking (2013),
available at http://www.ncee.org; Byron Auguste, Paul Kihn, and Matt
Miller, “Closing the Talent Gap: Attracting and Retaining Top-Third
Graduates to Careers in Teaching,” McKinsey & Company (Sept 2010). (The
McKinsey report has been criticized because it ranks the terciles by SAT
score/high school GPA, and only surveys a small population of new
teachers.) Thanks to Eric Kumbier for making this point to us in an
e-mail. / 50 <span class="bold1">Parental influence on kids’
education:</span> See, inter alia, Marianne Bertrand and Jessica Pan,
“The Trouble with Boys: Social Influences and the Gender Gap in
Disruptive Behavior,” <span class="ital">American</span>
<span class="ital">Economic Journal: Applied Economics</span> 5, no. 1
(2013); Shannon M. Pruden, Susan C. Levine, and Janellen Huttenlocher,
“Children’s Spatial Thinking: Does Talk About the Spatial World
Matter?,” <span class="ital">Developmental Science</span> 14 (November
2011); Bruce Sacerdote, “How Large Are the Effects from Changes in
Family Environment? A Study of Korean American Adoptees,”
<span class="ital">The Quarterly Journal of Economics</span> 122, no.1
(2007); Roland G. Fryer Jr. and Steven D. Levitt, “Understanding the
Black-White Test Score Gap in the First Two Years of School,”
<span class="ital">The Review of Economics and Statistics</span> 86, no.
2 (May 2004); Huttenlocher, Marina Vasilyeva, Elina Cymerman, and Susan
Levine, “Language Input and Child Syntax,” <span class="ital">Cognitive
Psychology</span> 45, no. 3 (2002). / 51 “<span class="bold1">Why do
American kids know less . . . ?”:</span> See 2012 report from Program
for International Student Assessment (PISA) / 51
<span class="bold1">Turn that child over . . . so the teachers can work
their magic:</span> for a rare example of a spirited argument in this
vein, see “The Depressing Data on Early Childhood Investment,” interview
with Jerome Kagan by Paul Solman, PBS.org (March 7, 2013).

52 <span class="bold4">THE LEGEND OF TAKERU KOBAYASHI</span>: We are
grateful to Kobi for the many hours of fascinating conversation
stretching over what turned out to be several years, and to all those
who helped facilitate those conversations, including Maggie James,
Noriko Okubo, Akiko Funatsu, Anna Berry, Kumi, and others. Kobi is so
convinced that competitive eating is an acquired skill that he says he
could train one of us to eat fifty HDB with just six months of training.
We have yet to take him up on this offer. Dubner did, however, get one
lesson from Kobi, at Gray’s Papaya in New York:

<div class="img-center">

<img src="images/00005.gif" class="calibre7" />

</div>

     We are indebted to the many journalists who have written about Kobi
and the sport of competitive eating, especially Jason Fagone, the author
of <span class="ital">Horsemen of the Esophagus: Competitive Eating and
the Big Fat American Dream</span> (Crown, 2006). Fagone steered us in
the right direction at the outset. We also drew upon: Fagone, “Dog Bites
Man,” Slate.com, July 8, 2010; Bill Belew, “Takeru ‘Tsunami’ Kobayashi
Training & Techniques to Defeat Joey Chestnut,” The Biz of Knowledge
website, June 29, 2007; “How Do You Speed Eat?” BBC News Magazine, July
4, 2006; Sarah Goldstein, “The Gagging and the Glory,” Salon.com, April
19, 2006; Josh Ozersky, “On Your Mark. Get Set. Pig Out,”
<span class="ital">New York,</span> June 26, 2005; Chris Ballard, “That
Is Going to Make You Money Someday,” <span class="ital">The New York
Times,</span> August 31, 2003; Associated Press, “Kobayashi’s Speedy
Gluttony Rattles Foes,” ESPN.com, July 4, 2001. / 53
<span class="bold1">Its promoters admit they concocted that
history:</span> See Sam Roberts, “No, He Did Not Invent the Publicity
Stunt,” <span class="ital">New York Times,</span> August 18, 2010. / 55
<span class="bold1">A schoolboy choked to death:</span> See Tama Miyake,
“Fast Food,” <span class="ital">Metropolis,</span> November 17, 2006. /
56 <span class="bold1">The opponent was a half-ton Kodiak bear:</span>
See Larry Getlen, “The Miracle That Is Kobayashi,”
<span class="ital">The Black Table</span> website, May 19, 2005. / 58
<span class="bold1">The hot-dog-bun challenge:</span> Thanks to the
Freakonomics Radio crew for trying this (and failing). As producer Greg
Rosalsky put it: “The first bun soaks up your saliva like a sponge and
then it seems virtually impossible to eat the second one.” / 61
“<span class="bold1">I wish there were hot dogs in jail”:</span> See
“Kobayashi Freed, Pleads Not Guilty,” ESPN.com News Services (with
reporting from the Associated Press), ESPN New York, July 5, 2010. / 63
<span class="bold1">Even elite athletes can be tricked:</span> See M. R.
Stone, K. Thomas, M. Wilkinson, A. M. Jones, A. St. Clair Gibson, and K.
G. Thompson, “Effects of Deception on Exercise Performance: Implications
for Determinants of Fatigue in Humans,” <span class="ital">Medicine &
Science in Sports & Exercise</span> 44, No. 3 (March 2012); Gina Kolata,
“A Little Deception Helps Push Athletes to the Limit,”
<span class="ital">New York Times</span>, September 19, 2011. Thanks
also to Kolata for the Roger Bannister quote, which we appropriated. /
64 “<span class="bold1">I can keep going”:</span> Thanks again to Jason
Fagone for this quote; it appeared in the May 2006 issue of
<span class="ital">The Atlantic,</span> in an excerpt from his
<span class="ital">Horsemen of the Esophagus</span> book.

<span class="bold1">CHAPTER 4: LIKE A BAD DYE JOB, THE TRUTH IS IN THE
ROOTS</span>

66 <span class="bold4">“STARVATION IS THE CHARACTERISTIC . . .”</span>:
See Amartya Sen, <span class="ital">Poverty and Famines: An Essay on
Entitlement and Deprivation</span> (Oxford Univ. Press, 1981). / 67
<span class="bold1">We throw away an astonishing 40 percent of the
food:</span> See “USDA and EPA Launch U.S. Food Waste Challenge,” USDA
new release, June 4, 2013.

67 <span class="bold4">THE RISE AND FALL IN VIOLENT CRIME</span>: See
Steven D. Levitt and Stephen J. Dubner,
<span class="ital">Freakonomics</span> (William Morrow, 2005); and
Levitt, “Understanding Why Crime Fell in the 1990s: Four Factors That
Explain the Decline and Six That Do Not,” <span class="ital">Journal of
Economic Perspectives</span> 18, no. 1 (winter 2004), pp. 163–190. / 68
<span class="bold1">The homicide rate today . . . lower than it was in
1960:</span> See Erica L. Smith and Alexia Cooper, “Homicide in the U.S.
Known to Law Enforcement, 2011,” Bureau of Justice Statistics (Dec.
2013); U.S. Department of Justice, Federal Bureau of Investigation,
“Crime in the United States, 2011,” Table 1; Barry Krisberg, Carolina
Guzman, Linh Vuong, “Crime and Economic Hard Times,” National Council on
Crime and Delinquency (February 2009); and James Alan Fox and Marianne
W. Zawitz, “Homicide Trends in the United States,” Bureau of Justice
Statistics (2007). / 69 <span class="bold1">The abortion-crime
link:</span> See Levitt and Dubner,
<span class="ital">Freakonomics</span> (William Morrow, 2005); and John
J. Donohue III and Levitt, “The Impact of Legalized Abortion on Crime,”
<span class="ital">The Quarterly Journal of Economics</span> 116, no. 2
(May 2001).

70 <span class="bold4">LET’S PRETEND YOU ARE A GERMAN FACTORY
WORKER</span>: See Jörg Spenkuch, “The Protestant Ethic and Work: Micro
Evidence From Contemporary Germany,” University of Chicago working
paper. Also based on author interviews with Spenkuch, and we are
grateful to Spenkuch for his comments on our manuscript. For further
recent evidence of the Protestant work ethic, see Andre van Hoorn,
Robbert Maseland, “Does a Protestant Work Ethic Exist? Evidence from the
Well-Being Effect of Unemployment,” <span class="ital">Journal of
Economic Behavior & Organization</span> 91 (July 2013). Meanwhile,
Davide Cantoni has argued that the Protestant ethic did not improve
economic outcomes in Germany; see Cantoni, “The Economic Effects of the
Protestant Reformation: Testing the Weber Hypothesis in the German
Lands,” job market paper, November 10, 2009. / 73 <span class="bold1">In
defense, however, of German Catholicism . . . (footnote):</span> See
Spenkuch and Philipp Tillmann, “Elite Influence? Religion, Economics,
and the Rise of the Nazis,” working paper, 2013.

73 <span class="bold4">WHY, FOR INSTANCE, ARE SOME ITALIAN TOWNS
. . .</span> : See Luigi Guiso, Paola Sapienza, and Luigi Zingales,
“Long-Term Persistence,” July 2013 working paper; see also earlier
versions by same authors: “Long-Term Cultural Persistence,” September
2012 working paper; and “Long-Term Persistence,” European University
Institute working paper 2008. Hat tip to Hans-Joachim Voth and Nico
Voigtländer, “Hatred Transformed: How Germans Changed Their Minds About
Jews, 1890–2006,” <span class="ital">Vox,</span> May 1, 2012.

74 <span class="bold4">ETHNIC STRIFE IN AFRICA</span>: See Stelios
Michalopoulos and Elias Papaioannou, “The Long-Run Effects of the
Scramble for Africa,” NBER working paper, November 2011; and Elliott
Green, “On the Size and Shape of African States,”
<span class="ital">International Studies Quarterly</span> 56, no. 2
(June 2012).

74 <span class="bold4">THE SCARS OF COLONIALISM STILL HAUNT SOUTH
AMERICA AS WELL</span>: See Melissa Dell, “The Persistent Effects of
Peru’s Mining <span class="ital">Mita,</span>” MIT working paper,
January 2010; and Daron Acemoglu, Camilo Garcia-Jimeno, and James A.
Robinson, “Finding Eldorado: Slavery and Long-Run Development in
Colombia,” NBER working paper, June 2012.

75 <span class="bold4">THE SALT-SENSITIVITY THEORY OF AFRICAN-AMERICAN
HYPERTENSION</span>: This section is based on author interview with
Roland Fryer as reflected in Stephen J. Dubner, “Toward a Unified Theory
of Black America,” <span class="ital">New York Times Magazine,</span>
March 20, 2005. We are also grateful for the excellent article by Mark
Warren in <span class="ital">Esquire,</span> “Roland Fryer’s Big Ideas”
(December 2005). See also: David M. Cutler, Roland G. Fryer Jr., and
Edward L. Glaeser, “Racial Differences in Life Expectancy: The Impact of
Salt, Slavery, and Selection,” unpublished manuscript, Harvard
University and NBER, March 1, 2005; and Katherine M. Barghaus, David M.
Cutler, Roland G. Fryer Jr., and Edward L. Glaeser, “An Empirical
Examination of Racial Differences in Health,” unpublished manuscript,
Harvard University, University of Pennsylvania, and NBER, November 2008.
For further background, see: Gary Taubes, “Salt, We Misjudged You,”
<span class="ital">The New York Times,</span> June 3, 2012; Nicholas
Bakalar, “Patterns: Less Salt Isn’t Always Better for the Heart,”
<span class="ital">The New York Time</span>s<span class="ital">,</span>
November 29, 2011; Martin J. O’Donnell et al., “Urinary Sodium and
Potassium Excretion and Risk of Cardiovascular Events,”
<span class="ital">The Journal of the American Medical
Association</span> 306, no. 20 (November 23/30, 2011); Michael H.
Alderman, “Evidence Relating Dietary Sodium to Cardiovascular Disease,”
<span class="ital">Journal of the American College of Nutrition</span>
25, no. 3 (2006); Jay Kaufman, “The Anatomy of a Medical Myth,”
<span class="ital">Is Race “Real”?</span>, SSRC Web Forum June 7, 2006;
Joseph E. Inikori and Stanley L. Engerman, <span class="ital">The
Atlantic Slave Trade: Effects on Economies, Societies and Peoples in
Africa, the Americas, and Europe</span> (Duke University Press, 1998);
and F. C. Luft et al., “Salt Sensitivity and Resistance of Blood
Pressure. Age and Race as Factors in Physiological Responses,”
<span class="ital">Hypertension</span> 17 (1991). / 75
“<span class="bold1">An Englishman Tastes the Sweat of an
African”:</span> Courtesy of the John Carter Brown Library at Brown
University. Original source: M. Chambon, <span class="ital">Le Commerce
de l’Amerique par Marseille</span> (Avignon, 1764), vol. 2, plate XI,
facing p. 400.

78 <span class="bold4">“WE LIVE IN AN AGE OF SCIENCE . . .”</span>: See
Roy Porter, <span class="ital">The Greatest Benefit to Mankind: A
Medical History of Humanity from Antiquity to the Present</span>
(HarperCollins, 1997).

78 <span class="bold4">CONSIDER THE ULCER</span>: The story of Barry
Marshall (and Robin Warren) is fascinating and heroic from start to end.
We strongly encourage you to read more about him, in any or all of the
following works cited, which also include more general information about
ulcers and the pharmaceutical industry. For the story of Marshall
himself, we were most reliant on a wonderful long interview conducted by
the estimable Norman Swan, an Australian physician who works as a
journalist. See Norman Swan, “Interviews with Australian Scientists:
Professor Barry Marshall,” Australian Academy of Science, 2008. Thanks
to Dr. Marshall himself for offering his useful comments on what we
wrote about him here and in Chapter 5. We are also indebted to: Kathryn
Schulz, “Stress Doesn’t Cause Ulcers! Or, How to Win a Nobel Prize in
One Easy Lesson: Barry Marshall on Being . . . Right,” Slate.com,
September 9, 2010; Pamela Weintraub, “The Dr. Who Drank Infectious
Broth, Gave Himself an Ulcer, and Solved a Medical Mystery,”
<span class="ital">Discover,</span> March 2010; and “Barry J. Marshall,
Autobiography,” The Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine 2005,
Nobelprize.org, 2005. / 79 <span class="bold1">The first true
blockbuster drugs:</span> See Melody Petersen, <span class="ital">Our
Daily Meds: How the Pharmaceutical Companies Transformed Themselves into
Slick Marketing Machines and Hooked the Nation on Prescription
Drugs</span> (Sarah Crichton Books, 2008); and Shannon Brownlee, “Big
Pharma’s Golden Eggs,” <span class="ital">Washington Post,</span> April
6, 2008; “Having an Ulcer Is Getting a Lot Cheaper,”
<span class="ital">BusinessWeek,</span> May 8, 1994. / 79
<span class="bold1">In the past, some medical researcher might have
suggested . . . :</span> In particular we are thinking of Dr. A. Stone
Freedberg of Harvard, who in 1940 published a paper “identifying similar
bacteria in 40 percent of patients with ulcers and stomach cancer”; see
Lawrence K. Altman, “Two Win Nobel Prize for Discovering Bacterium Tied
to Stomach Ailments,” <span class="ital">The New York Times</span>,
October 4, 2005; and Lawrence K. Altman, “A Scientist, Gazing Toward
Stockholm, Ponders ‘What If?,’ “ <span class="ital">New York
Times,</span> December 6, 2005. / 82 <span class="bold1">Even today,
many people still believe that ulcers are caused by stress . . .
:</span> Perhaps they are still swayed by New York City’s famously
feisty mayor Ed Koch. “I’m the sort of person who will never get
ulcers,” he once said. “Why? Because I say exactly what I think. I’m the
sort of person who might give other people ulcers.” See Maurice Carroll,
“How’s He Doing? How’s He Doing?,” <span class="ital">New York
Times,</span> December 24, 1978.

85 <span class="bold4">THE POWER OF POOP</span>: This section was based
primarily on author interviews with the gastroenterologists Thomas
Borody, Alexander Khoruts, and Michael Levitt (father of Steve Levitt),
as reflected in Stephen J. Dubner, “The Power of Poop,” Freakonomics
Radio, March 4, 2011. We are also grateful to Borody for offering useful
comments to this section. See also: Borody, Sudarshan Paramsothy, and
Gaurav Agrawal, “Fecal Microbiota Transplantation: Indications, Methods,
Evidence, and Future Directions,” <span class="ital">Current
Gastroenterology Reports</span> 15, no. 337 (July 2013); W. H. Wilson
Tang et al., “Intestinal Microbial Metabolism of Phosphatidylcholine and
Cardiovascular Risk,” <span class="ital">New England Journal of
Medicine</span> 368, no. 17 (April 2013); Olga C. Aroniadis and Lawrence
J. Brandt, “Fecal Microbiota Transplantation: Past, Present and Future,”
<span class="ital">Current Opinion in Gastroenterology</span> 29, no. 1
(January 2013); “Jonathan Eisen: Meet Your Microbes,” TEDMED Talk,
Washington, D.C., April 2012; Borody and Khoruts, “Fecal Microbiota
Transplantation and Emerging Applications,” <span class="ital">Nature
Reviews Gastroenterology & Hepatology</span> 9, no. 2 (2011); Khoruts et
al., “Changes in the Composition of the Human Fecal Microbiome After
Bacteriotherapy for Recurrent <span class="ital">Clostridium
Difficile</span>–Associated Diarrhea,” <span class="ital">Journal of
Clinical Gastroenterology</span> 44, no. 5 (May/June 2010); Borody et
al., “Bacteriotherapy Using Fecal Flora: Toying with Human Motions,”
<span class="ital">Journal of Clinical Gastroenterology</span> 38, no. 6
(July 2004). / 85 <span class="bold1">Looks like chocolate milk:</span>
That is according to Josbert Keller, a gastroenterologist at the
HagaZiekenhuis hospital in the Hague, an author of “Duodenal Infusion of
Donor Feces for Recurrent <span class="ital">Clostridium
difficile</span>,” <span class="ital">New England Journal of
Medicine</span> 368 (2013):407–415; see also Denise Grady, “When Pills
Fail, This, er, Option Provides a Cure,” <span class="ital">New York
Times,</span> January 16, 2013. / 85 <span class="bold1">Colitis
“previously an incurable disease”:</span> See Borody and Jordana
Campbell, “Fecal Microbiota Transplantation: Techniques, Applications,
and Issues,” <span class="ital">Gastroenterology Clinics of North
America</span> 41 (2012); and Borody, Eloise F. Warren, Sharyn Leis,
Rosa Surace, and Ori Ashman, “Treatment of Ulcerative Colitis Using
Fecal Bacteriotherapy,” <span class="ital">Journal of Clinical
Gastroenterology</span> 37, no. 1 (July 2003).

<span class="bold1">CHAPTER 5: THINK LIKE A CHILD</span>

88 <span class="bold4">“SOPHISTICATION” AND THE SOPHISTS
(FOOTNOTE)</span>: Drawn from the “Sophisticated” entry on
worldwidewords.org, by the excellent British etymologist Michael
Quinion.

89 <span class="bold4">“TO EXPLAIN ALL NATURE IS TOO DIFFICULT A TASK
. . .”</span>: See Isaac Newton and J. E. McGuire, “Newton’s ‘Principles
of Philosophy’: An Intended Preface for the 1704 ‘Opticks’ and a Related
Draft Fragment,” <span class="ital">The British Journal for the History
of Science</span> 5, no. 2 (December 1970); hat tip to Freakonomics
Radio producer Katherine Wells, who scripted this for Stephen J. Dubner,
“The Truth Is Out There . . . Isn’t It?,” Freakonomics Radio, November
23, 2011.

90 <span class="bold4">DRUNK WALKING</span>: See Steven D. Levitt and
Stephen J. Dubner, <span class="ital">SuperFreakonomics</span> (William
Morrow, 2009) / 90 <span class="bold1">Mom-and-pop bagel-delivery
outfit:</span> Levitt and Dubner, <span class="ital">Freakonomics</span>
(William Morrow, 2005) / 91 <span class="bold1">Guns versus swimming
pools:</span> Levitt and Dubner, <span class="ital">Freakonomics</span>.

91 <span class="bold4">POOR VISION AND CLASSROOM PERFORMANCE</span>: See
Stephen J. Dubner, “Smarter Kids at 10 Bucks a Pop,” Freakonomics Radio,
April 8, 2011. This report was based primarily on author interviews with
Paul Glewwe and Albert Park and drew on their paper “Visualizing
Development: Eyeglasses and Academic Performance in Rural Primary
Schools in China,” University of Minnesota Center for International Food
and Agricultural Policy, working paper WP12-2 (2012), coauthored with
Meng Zhao. See also: Douglas Heingartner, “Better Vision for the World,
on a Budget,” <span class="ital">New York Times,</span> January 2, 2010;
and “Comprehensive Eye Exams Particularly Important for Classroom
Success,” American Optometric Association (2008). For the “four-eyes”
stigma and “planos” (in footnote), see Dubner, “Playing the Nerd Card,”
Freakonomics Radio, May 31, 2012.

93 <span class="bold4">AS ALBERT EINSTEIN LIKED TO SAY . . .</span> :
Thanks again to Garson O’Toole at QuoteInvestigator.com.

94 <span class="bold4">LET’S RETURN BRIEFLY TO BARRY MARSHALL</span>:
Once again, we drew heavily from the excellent interview of Marshall
conducted by Norman Swan, “Interviews with Australian Scientists:
Professor Barry Marshall,” Australian Academy of Science, 2008.

96 <span class="bold4">EXPERT PERFORMANCE</span>: See, for starters,
Stephen J. Dubner and Steven D. Levitt, “A Star Is Made,”
<span class="ital">The New York Times Magazine,</span> May 7, 2006. Our
enduring thanks to K. Anders Ericsson; his work and that of his many
fascinating colleagues is well represented in Ericsson, Neil Charness,
Paul J. Feltovich, and Robert R. Hoffman, <span class="ital">The
Cambridge Handbook of Expertise and Expert Performance</span> (Cambridge
University Press, 2006). For related books on the topic, see Daniel
Coyle, <span class="ital">The Talent Code</span> (Bantam, 2009); Geoff
Colvin, <span class="ital">Talent Is Overrated</span> (Portfolio, 2008);
and Malcolm Gladwell, <span class="ital">Outliers</span> (Little, Brown
& Co., 2008).

98 <span class="bold4">PRIZE-LINKED SAVINGS</span>: For a fuller
treatment of this topic, see Stephen J. Dubner, “Could a Lottery Be the
Answer to America’s Poor Savings Rate?,” Freakonomics Radio, November
18, 2010; and Dubner, “Who Could Say No to a ‘No-Lose Lottery?,’ “
Freakonomics Radio, Dec. 2, 2010. These episodes featured interviews
with, among many others, Melissa S. Kearney and Peter Tufano, both of
whom are extremely knowledgeable about the topic. See, e.g., Kearney,
Tufano, Jonathan Guryan, and Erik Hurst, “Making Savers Winners: An
Overview of Prize-Linked Saving Products,” in Olivia S. Mitchell and
Annamaria Lusardi (eds.), <span class="ital">Financial Literacy:
Implications for Retirement Security and the Financial
Marketplace</span> (Oxford University Press, 2011).

101 <span class="bold4"> KIDS ARE HARDER TO FOOL WITH MAGIC</span>: The
Alex Stone section was based primarily on author interviews. See also
<span class="ital">Fooling Houdini: Magicians, Mentalists, Math Geeks,
and the Hidden Powers of the Mind</span> (HarperCollins, 2012); and
Steven D. Levitt, “<span class="ital">Fooling Houdini</span> Author Alex
Stone Answers Your Questions,” Freakonomics.com, July 23, 2012. On the
point of “paying attention,” Stone acknowledges the insights of the
developmental psychologist Alison Gopnik, author of
<span class="ital">The Philosophical Baby: What Children’s Minds Tell Us
About Truth, Love, and the Meaning of Life</span> (Farrar, Straus and
Giroux, 2009). For further reading on children and illusion, see Bruce
Bower, “Adults Fooled by Visual Illusion, But Not Kids,”
<span class="ital">ScienceNews</span> via Wired.com, November 23, 2009;
and Vincent H. Gaddis, “The Art of Honest Deception,” StrangeMag.com.

104 <span class="bold4"> ISAAC BASHEVIS SINGER WRITING FOR KIDS</span>:
See Singer, “Why I Write for Children,” prepared for a 1970
award-acceptance speech, read at his 1978 Nobel acceptance speech, and
reprinted in Singer, <span class="ital">Nobel Lecture</span> (Farrar,
Straus & Giroux, 1979). Thanks to Jonathan Rosen for bringing this
(along with many other good things) to our attention.

<span class="bold1">CHAPTER 6: LIKE GIVING CANDY TO A BABY</span>

105 <span class="bold4"> AMANDA AND THE M&M’S</span>: A charming
animated version of this story appears in
<span class="ital">Freakonomics: The Movie</span>. Chad Troutwine was
the lead producer of the film; the director Seth Gordon headed up the
team that created the Amanda section.

107 <span class="bold4"> THE AVERAGE U.S. ADULT WEIGHS ABOUT 25 POUNDS
MORE TODAY THAN A FEW DECADES AGO</span>: See Centers for Disease
Control, “Mean Body Weight, Height, and Body Mass Index, United States
1960–2002”; USDA, “Profiling Food Consumption in America,” chapter 2 in
the <span class="ital">Agriculture Factbook 2001-2002</span>; USDA
“Percent of Household Final Consumption Expenditures Spent on Food,
Alcoholic Beverages, and Tobacco That Were Consumed at Home, by Selected
Countries, 2012,” ERS Food Expenditure Series. / 107
<span class="bold1">Why have we gotten so fat?:</span> There is a large
and sometimes confusing literature on the relationship between food and
price, with considerable dissent over the methodology of measuring food
costs. Some researchers, for instance, take issue with the
cost-per-calorie method. Two examples: Fred Kuchler and Hayden Stewart,
“Price Trends Are Similar for Fruits, Vegetables, and Snack Foods,”
Report ERR-55, USDA Economic Research Service; and Andrea Carlson and
Elizabeth Frazao, “Are Healthy Foods Really More Expensive? It Depends
on How You Measure the Price,” <span class="ital">USDA Economic
Information Bulletin</span> 96 (May 2012). Among the research that most
closely represents what we’ve written in this chapter, see: Michael
Grossman, Erdal Tekin, and Roy Wada, “Food Prices and Body Fatness Among
Youths,” NBER working paper, June 2013; Stephen J. Dubner, “100 Ways to
Fight Obesity,” Freakonomics Radio, March 27, 2013; Pablo Monsivais and
Adam Drewnowski, “The Rising Cost of Low-Energy-Density Foods,”
<span class="ital">Journal of the American Dietetic Association</span>
107, no. 12 (December 2007); Tara Parker-Pope, “A High Price for Healthy
Food,” <span class="ital">The New York Times</span> (Well blog),
December 5, 2007; Cynthia L. Ogden, Cheryl D. Fryar, Margaret D.
Carroll, and Katherine M. Flegal, “Mean Body Weight, Height, and Body
Mass Index, United States 1960–2002,” <span class="ital">Advance Data
from Vital and Health Statistics</span> 347 (National Center for Health
Statistics, 2004); David M. Cutler, Edward L. Glaeser, and Jesse M.
Shapiro, “Why Have Americans Become More Obese?”
<span class="ital">Journal of Economic Perspectives</span> 17, no. 3
(Summer 2003).

108 <span class="bold4"> CONSIDER A 2011 TRAFFIC ACCIDENT</span>: See
Josh Tapper, “Did Chinese Laws Keep Strangers from Helping Toddler Hit
by Truck,” <span class="ital">The (Toronto) Star,</span> October 18,
2011; Li Wenfang, “Hospital Offers Little Hope for Girl’s Survival,”
<span class="ital">China Daily,</span> October 17, 2011; Michael Wines,
“Bystanders’ Neglect of Injured Toddler Sets Off Soul-Searching on Web
Sites in China,” <span class="ital">New York Times,</span> October 11,
2011. Thanks to Robert Alan Greevy for bringing this story to our
attention.

109 <span class="bold4"> CASH FOR GRADES</span>: See Steven D. Levitt,
John A. List, Susanne Neckermann, and Sally Sadoff, “The Impact of
Short-Term Incentives on Student Performance,” University of Chicago
working paper, September 2011; and Roland G. Fryer Jr., “Financial
Incentives and Student Achievement: Evidence from Randomized Trials,”
<span class="ital">The Quarterly Journal of Economics</span> 126, no. 4
(2011).

112 <span class="bold4"> ROBERT CIALDINI’S EXPERIMENTS WITH ELECTRICITY
CONSUMPTION AND PETRIFIED-WOOD THEFT</span>: Drawn from author
interviews with Cialdini as reflected in Stephen J. Dubner, “Riding the
Herd Mentality,” Freakonomics Radio, June 21, 2012. Cialdini’s book
<span class="ital">Influence</span> is a fantastic introduction to his
way of thinking. See also: Jessica M. Nolan, P. Wesley Schultz, Robert
B. Cialdini, Noah J. Goldstein, and Vladas Griskevicius, “Normative
Social Influence Is Underdetected,” <span class="ital">Personality and
Social Psychology Bulletin</span> 34, no. 913 (2008); Goldstein,
Cialdini, and Steve Martin, <span class="ital">Yes!: 50 Secrets from the
Science of Persuasion</span> (Free Press, 2008); Schultz, Nolan,
Cialdini, Goldstein, and Griskevicius, “The Constructive, Destructive,
and Reconstructive Power of Social Norms,”
<span class="ital">Psychological Science</span> 18, no. 5 (2007);
Cialdini, Linda J. Demaine, Brad J. Sagarin, Daniel W. Barrett, Kelton
Rhoads, and Patricia L. Winter, “Managing Social Norms for Persuasive
Impact,” <span class="ital">Social Influence</span> 1, no. 1 (2006);
Cialdini, “Crafting Normative Messages to Protect the Environment,”
<span class="ital">Current Directions in Psychological Science</span> 12
(2003). In the petrified-wood study, there were other sign options,
including one that showed a park visitor stealing wood, accompanied by
the message “Please don’t remove petrified wood from the park.” This
sign did outperform the no-sign option.

117 <span class="bold4"> BRIAN MULLANEY, SMILE TRAIN, AND
“ONCE-AND-DONE”</span>: This section was drawn primarily from author
interviews with Mullaney, an unpublished memoir by Mullaney, and the
research reflected in Amee Kamdar, Steven D. Levitt, John A. List, and
Chad Syverson, “Once and Done: Leveraging Behavioral Economics to
Increase Charitable Contributions,” University of Chicago working paper,
2013. See also: Stephen J. Dubner and Levitt, “Bottom-Line
Philanthropy,” <span class="ital">New York Times Magazine,</span> March
9, 2008; and James Andreoni, “Impure Altruism and Donations to Public
Goods: A Theory of Warm-Glow Giving,” <span class="ital">The Economic
Journal</span> 100, no. 401 (June 1990). For another version of the
“once-and-done” story, see Uri Gneezy and List, <span class="ital">The
Why Axis: Hidden Motives and the Undiscovered Economics of Everyday
Life</span> (Public Affairs, 2013). / 119 <span class="bold1">Peter
Buffett and “conscience laundering”:</span> See Peter Buffett, “The
Charitable-Industrial Complex,” <span class="ital">New York
Times,</span> July 26, 2013. For a related conversation with Buffett, on
the topic of his having won “the ovarian lottery”—he is a son of Warren
Buffett—see Dubner, “Growing Up Buffett,” May 13, 2011.

127 <span class="bold4"> ENTER THE PING-PONG TEAMS</span>: See Henry A.
Kissinger, <span class="ital">On China</span> (Penguin, 2011);
“Ping-Pong Diplomacy (April 6–17, 1971),”
<span class="ital">AmericanExperience.com</span>; David A. DeVoss,
“Ping-Pong Diplomacy,” <span class="ital">Smithsonian,</span> April
2002; “The Ping Heard Round the World,” <span class="ital">Time,</span>

128 <span class="bold4"> ZAPPOS</span>: This section was based in part
on author interviews with Tony Hsieh and a visit to Zappos headquarters.

131 <span class="bold4"> HFC-23 AND PAYING POLLUTERS TO POLLUTE</span>:

Lehren, “Profits on Carbon Credits Drive Output of a Harmful Gas,”
<span class="ital">New York Times,</span> August 8, 2012.

133 <span class="bold4"> “THE COBRA EFFECT”</span>: See Stephen J.

Guardian</span> (South Africa), October 26, 2012. / 133
<span class="bold1">As Mark Twain once wrote . . . :</span> See Mark

from the North American Review</span>, ed. Michael Kiskis (University of
Wisconsin Press, 1990). We are grateful to Jared Morton for bringing

<span class="bold1">CHAPTER 7: WHAT DO KING SOLOMON AND DAVID LEE ROTH
HAVE IN COMMON?</span>

137 <span class="bold4"> KING SOLOMON:</span> The biblical quotes here
are from <span class="ital">The Tanakh</span> (Jewish Publication
Societies, 1917). The story of Solomon and the maternity dispute can be
found beginning at 1 Kings 3:16. We also relied on Rabbi Joseph
Telushkin, <span class="ital">Biblical Literacy</span> (William Morrow,
1997). There is a great deal of commentary surrounding this story, as
there is with many biblical tales. For a good modern summary, which
includes ancient commentary, see Mordecai Kornfeld, “King Solomon’s
Wisdom,” <span class="ital">Rabbi Mordecai Kornfeld’s Weekly
Parasha-Page</span>; and Baruch C. Cohen, “The Brilliant Wisdom of King
Solomon,” <span class="ital">Jewish Law Commentary,</span> July 10,
1998. Both of these interpretations stress the incentives presented by
<span class="ital">yibbum, “</span>a rite which must be performed when a
man who has a living brother dies childless.” The Solomon story has also
been dissected by nonbiblical scholars, including the economists Avinash
K. Dixit and Barry J. Nalebuff in <span class="ital">The Art of
Strategy</span> (Norton, 2008). Dixit and Nalebuff approach the story as
a game-theory puzzle and conclude that the second woman erred in
agreeing with King Solomon to divide the child in half. Indeed, why
would the second woman go to the trouble to steal the baby and then so
blithely agree to have it killed? Also, once the first woman renounced
ownership, why wouldn’t the second woman simply keep quiet and accept
the baby? By this reckoning, Solomon “was more lucky than wise,” write
Dixit and Nalebuff. “\[H\]is strategy worked only because of the second
woman’s error.” The economists’ interpretation, we should note, relies
on a literality that many biblical scholars are careful to avoid in
pursuit of less utilitarian insights.

138 <span class="bold4"> DAVID LEE ROTH</span>: See Jane Rocca, “What I
Know About Women,” <span class="ital">Brisbane Times,</span> April 7,
2013; David Lee Roth, “Brown M&Ms,” online video clip on Van Halen’s
Vimeo channel, 2012; Scott R. Benarde, <span class="ital">Stars of
David: Rock ’n’ Roll’s Jewish Stories</span> (Brandeis University Press,
2003); David Lee Roth, <span class="ital">Crazy from the Heat</span>
(Hyperion, 1997); Mikal Gilmore, “The Endless Party,”
<span class="ital">Rolling Stone,</span> September 4, 1980. Portions of
the Van Halen rider are posted on TheSmokingGun.com; special thanks to
Mike Peden for verifying the Van Halen rider details, via the files of
Jack Belle.

144 <span class="bold4"> MEDIEVAL ORDEALS</span>: See Peter T. Leeson,
“Ordeals,” <span class="ital">Journal of Law and Economics</span> 55
(August 2012). For further Leeson reading, see “Gypsy Law,”
<span class="ital">Public Choice</span> 155 (June 2013);
<span class="ital">The Invisible Hook: The Hidden Economics of
Pirates</span> (Princeton Univ. Press, 2009);
“An-<span class="ital">arrgh</span>-chy: The Law and Economics of Pirate
Organization,” <span class="ital">Journal of Political Economy</span>
115, no. 6 (2007); and “Trading with Bandits,”
<span class="ital">Journal of Law and Economics</span> 50 (May 2007). We
are grateful to Leeson for his helpful comments on our manuscript.

149 <span class="bold4"> THE HIGH COST OF EMPLOYEE TURNOVER</span>: See
Mercer and the National Retail Federation, “U.S. Retail Compensation and
Benefits Survey,” October 2013; Jordan Melnick, “Hiring’s New Frontier,”
QSRmagazine.com, September 2012; and Melnick, “More Than Minimum Wage,”
QSRmagazine.com, November 2011.

150 <span class="bold4"> A WORKER WITH A FOUR-YEAR DEGREE EARNS ABOUT 75
PERCENT MORE</span>: See “Education at a Glance 2013: OECD Indicators”
(OECD, 2013).

150 <span class="bold4"> ZAPPOS AND “THE OFFER”</span>: See Stephen J.
Dubner, “The Upside of Quitting,” September 30, 2011; Stacey Vanek-Smith
conducted the interview with Tony Hsieh and other Zappos employees.
Thanks to various Zappos employees for follow-up interviews. / 151
<span class="bold1">It costs an average of roughly \$4,000 to replace a

/ 151 <span class="bold1">A single bad hire can cost . . . :</span>
Drawn from a CareerBuilder survey by Harris Interactive.

152 <span class="bold4"> THE SECRET BULLET FACTORY AND THE WARM-BEER
ALARM</span>: Based primarily on author visit to the site, with
follow-up correspondence with Yehudit Ayalon. See also: Eli Sa’adi,
<span class="ital">The Ayalon Institute: Kibbutzim Hill—Rehovot</span>
(pamphlet, available on-site).

154 <span class="bold4"> WHY DO NIGERIAN SCAMMERS SAY THEY ARE FROM
NIGERIA?</span> This section was drawn from author interviews with
Cormac Herley and from Herley’s fascinating paper “Why Do Nigerian
Scammers Say They Are from Nigeria?,” Workshop on Economics of

bringing Herley’s paper to our attention. / 154 <span class="bold1">Dear
Sir/Madam, TOP SECRET:</span> This letter is a mashup of various scam

<span class="bold1">Firm numbers are hard to come by:</span> For overall

Information Security, Berlin, Germany, June 26, 2012; and Internet Crime

<span class="bold1">One California victim lost \$5 million:</span> See
Onell R. Soto, “Fight to Get Money Back a Loss,” <span class="ital">San

<span class="bold1">Roughly 95 percent of the burglar alarms . . . are

<span class="ital">Problem-Oriented Guides for Police: False Burglar

Emergency Calls,” <span class="ital">Evaluation and Program

161 <span class="bold4"> WHY TERRORISTS</span>
<span class="boldital">SHOULDN’T</span> <span class="bold4">BUY LIFE

<span class="bold1">CHAPTER 8: HOW TO PERSUADE PEOPLE WHO DON’T WANT TO
BE PERSUADED</span>

167 <span class="bold4"> FIRST, UNDERSTAND HOW HARD THIS WILL BE</span>:
Much of this section is drawn from the work of the Cultural Cognition
Project and author interviews with Dan Kahan and Ellen Peters as
presented in Stephen J. Dubner, “The Truth Is Out There . . . Isn’t
It?,” Freakonomics Radio, November 30, 2011. The CCP’s website is an
excellent resource for their work. For the climate-change topic, see
Kahan, Peters, Maggie Wittlin, Paul Slovic, Lisa Larrimore Ouellette,
Donald Braman, and Gregory Mandel, “The Polarizing Impact of Science
Literacy and Numeracy on Perceived Climate Change Risks,
<span class="ital">Nature Climate Change</span> 2 (2012). (For an
earlier version of that paper, see Kahan et al., “The Tragedy of the
Risk-Perception Commons: Culture Conflict, Rationality Conflict, and
Climate Change,” Cultural Cognition Project working paper no. 89.
Further information on the numeracy and science-literacy questions can
be found in these papers as well as in Joshua A. Weller et al.,
“Development and Testing of an Abbreviated Numeracy Scale: A Rasch
Analysis Approach,” <span class="ital">Journal of Behavioral Decision
Making</span> 26 (2012). / 168 <span class="bold1">The vast majority of
climate scientists believe the world is getting hotter:</span> See,
e.g., Chris D. Thomas et al., “Extinction Risk from Climate Change,”
<span class="ital">Nature</span> 427 (January 2004); Camille Parmesan
and Gary Yohe, “A Globally Coherent Fingerprint of Climate Change
Impacts Across Natural Systems,” <span class="ital">Nature</span> 421
(January 2003); Gian-Reto Walther et al., “Ecological Responses to
Recent Climate Change,” <span class="ital">Nature</span> 416 (March
2002); and Peter M. Cox et al., “Acceleration of Global Warming Due to
Carbon-Cycle Feedbacks in a Coupled Climate Model,”
<span class="ital">Nature</span> 408 (November 2000). / 168
<span class="bold1">But the American public is far less
concerned:</span> See John Cook et al., “Quantifying the Consensus on
Anthropogenic Global Warming in the Scientific Literature,”
<span class="ital">Environmental Research Letters</span> 8, no. 2 (May
2013). / 168 <span class="bold1">Pew polls and attitudes about
scientists:</span> See Pew Research Center for the People & the Press,
“Public Praises Science; Scientists Fault Public, Media” (2009, Pew
Research Center). / 171 <span class="bold1">Terrorists, for example,
tend to be significantly better educated than their peers:</span> See
Alan B. Krueger, <span class="ital">What Makes a Terrorist</span>
(Princeton University Press, 2007); Claude Berrebi, “Evidence About the
Link Between Education, Poverty and Terrorism Among Palestinians,”
Princeton University Industrial Relations Section working paper, 2003;
and Krueger and Jita Maleckova, “Education, Poverty and Terrorism: Is
There a Causal Connection?” <span class="ital">Journal of Economic
Perspectives</span> 17, no. 4 (Fall 2003). / 172
<span class="bold1">Trying to keep a public men’s room clean?:</span>
See Richard H. Thaler and Cass R. Sunstein,
<span class="ital">Nudge</span> (Yale University Press, 2008). / 172
“<span class="bold1">. . . We are also blind to our blindness”:</span>
See Daniel Kahneman, <span class="ital">Thinking, Fast and Slow</span>
(2011, Farrar, Straus and Giroux). / 173 “<span class="bold1">It’s
easier to jump out of a plane”:</span> Kareem Abdul-Jabbar, “20 Things
Boys Can Do to Become Men,” Esquire.com, October 2013.

173 <span class="bold4"> HOW MUCH DID THE ANTI-DRUG CAMPAIGN CUT DRUG
USE?</span>: See Robert Hornik, Lela Jacobsohn, Robert Orwin, Andrea
Piesse, Graham Kalton, “Effects of the National Youth Anti-Drug Media
Campaign on Youths,” <span class="ital">American Journal of Public
Health</span> 98, no. 12 (December 2008).

174 <span class="bold4"> SELF-DRIVING CARS</span>: Among the many people
who informed our thinking on the driverless-car future, we are
especially indebted to Raj Rajkumar and his colleagues at Carnegie
Mellon, who let us ride in their driverless vehicle and answered every
question. / 175 <span class="bold1">Google has already driven its fleet
of autonomous cars:</span> See Angela Greiling Keane, “Google’s
Self-Driving Cars Get Boost from U.S. Agency,” Bloomberg.com, May 30,
2013; “The Self-Driving Car Logs More Miles on New Wheels,” Google
official blog, August 7, 2012. (Our text contains updated mile figures
from a Google spokesperson as of October 2013.) / 174
<span class="bold1">Ninety percent of traffic deaths due to driver
error:</span> Per Bob Joop Goos, chairman of the International
Organization for Road Accident Prevention; also per National Highway
Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) statistics. / 174
<span class="bold1">Worldwide traffic deaths:</span> Most of the
statistics in this section are drawn from World Health Organization and
NHTSA reports. / 175 <span class="bold1">In many U.S. cities, 30 to 40
percent of the downtown surface area is devoted to parking:</span> See
Stephen J. Dubner, “Parking Is Hell,” Freakonomics Radio, March 13,
2013; Donald Shoup, <span class="ital">The High Cost of Free
Parking</span> (American Planning Association, 2011); Eran Ben-Joseph,
<span class="ital">ReThinking a Lot: The Design and Culture of
Parking</span> (Massachusetts Institute of Technology, 2012); Catherine
Miller, <span class="ital">Carscape: A Parking Handbook</span>
(Washington Street Press, 1988); John A. Jakle and Keith A. Sculle,
<span class="ital">Lots of Parking: Land Use in a Car Culture</span>
(University of Virginia, 2004). / 176 <span class="bold1">Nearly 3
percent of the U.S. workforce . . . feed their families by
driving:</span> From a May 2012 Bureau of Labor Statistics report. The
largest single category is heavy trucks and tractor-trailers, with more
than 1.5 million drivers. / 178 <span class="bold1">In wealthy
countries, this is easily the leading cause of death for kids:</span>
Per the World Health Organization, the share of traffic deaths is lower
in less-developed countries, where many children die from pneumonia,
diarrhea, and the like. / 179 <span class="bold1">During this period of
zero airline deaths, more than 140,000 Americans died in traffic
crashes:</span> See Stephen J. Dubner, “One Thought About the Two Deaths
in Asiana Airlines Flight 214,” Freakonomics.com, July 8, 2013. For the
difference between car and plane travel as discussed in the footnote, we
relied on statistics from the Federal Highway Administration (for car
data) and the Bureau of Transportation Statistics (for airplane data).

180 <span class="bold4"> HAVE WE MENTIONED THAT NAME-CALLING IS A REALLY
BAD IDEA IF YOU WANT TO PERSUADE SOMEONE?</span>: Among the most
accomplished name-callers in the modern era is the
<span class="ital">New York Times</span> opinion columnist Paul Krugman.
A political liberal, he has referred to conservatives as “mean-spirited
class warriors” who are “wrong about everything,” who “quite literally
have no idea what they’re doing,” and “have transitioned from being the
stupid party to being the crazy party”—all in just three weeks’ worth of
columns. / 180 <span class="bold1">Negative information “weighs more
heavily on the brain”:</span> See Tiffany A. Ito, Jeff T. Larsen, N.
Kyle Smith, and John T. Cacioppi, “Negative Information Weighs More
Heavily on the Brain: The Negativity Bias in Evaluative
Categorizations,” <span class="ital">Journal of Personality and Social
Psychology</span> 75, no. 4 (1998). / 180 “<span class="bold1">Bad is
stronger than good”:</span> See Roy F. Baumeister, Ellen Bratslavsky,
Catrin Finkenauer, Kathleen D. Vohs, “Bad Is Stronger Than Good,”
<span class="ital">Review of General Psychology</span> 5, no. 4 (2001).
For more on this subject from Vohs, see Stephen J. Dubner, “Legacy of a
Jerk,” Freakonomics Radio, July 19, 2012. / 180
<span class="bold1">Negative events . . . make an outsize impression on
our memories:</span> As the late, great historian Barbara Tuchman wrote
in <span class="ital">A Distant Mirror: The Calamitous 14th
Century</span> (Knopf, 1978): “Disaster is rarely as pervasive as it
seems from recorded accounts. The fact of being on the record makes it
appear continuous and ubiquitous whereas it is more likely to have been
sporadic both in time and place. Besides, persistence of the normal is
usually greater than the effect of the disturbance, as we know from our
own times. After absorbing the news of today, one expects to face a
world consisting entirely of strikes, crimes, power failures, broken
water mains, stalled trains, school shutdowns, muggers, drug addicts,
neo-Nazis, and rapists. The fact is that one can come home in the
evening—on a lucky day—without having encountered more than one or two
of these phenomena. This has led me to formulate Tuchman’s Law, as
follows: ‘The fact of being reported multiplies the apparent extent of
any deplorable development by five- to tenfold’ (or any figure the
reader would care to supply).” / 180 <span class="bold1">Consider a
recent study of German schoolteachers:</span> See Thomas Unterbrink et
al., “Parameters Influencing Health Variables in a Sample of 949 German
Teachers,” <span class="ital">International Archives of Occupational and
Environmental Health,</span> May 2008.

182 <span class="bold4"> IF BEING FAT IS A BAD THING, THEN EATING FAT
MUST ALSO BE BAD</span>: See, among many others, Robert H. Lustig,
<span class="ital">Fat Chance: Beating the Odds Against Sugar, Processed
Food, Obesity, and Disease</span> (Hudson Street Press, 2012); and the
research of Dr. Peter Attia of the Nutrition Science Initiative as
discussed in Stephen J. Dubner, “100 Ways to Fight Obesity,”
Freakonomics Radio, March 27, 2013.

184 <span class="bold4"> THE ENCYCLOPEDIA OF ETHICAL FAILURE</span>:
Author interviews with Steve Epstein and Jeff Green, as featured in
Stephen J. Dubner, “Government Employees Gone Wild,” Freakonomics Radio,
July 18, 2013. See <span class="ital">Encyclopedia of Ethical
Failure</span>, Dept. of Defense, Office of General Counsel, Standards
of Conduct Office (July 2012); <span class="ital">Encyclopedia of
Ethical Failure: 2013 Updates</span>, same publisher; and Jonathan Karp,
“At the Pentagon, an ‘Encyclopedia of Ethical Failure,’ ”
<span class="ital">Wall Street Journal,</span> May 14, 2007.

185 <span class="bold4"> THE TEN COMMANDMENTS</span>: This version of
the Ten Commandments is drawn from the Jewish Publication Society’s 1917
English Translation of the Tanakh, with an assist from the version
contained in Joseph Telushkin, <span class="ital">Jewish Literacy</span>
(William Morrow, 1991). Throughout history and among different religious
groups, the Ten Commandments have been rendered in a variety of ways due
to differences in translation, interpretation, length, and the fact that
they appear twice in the Torah, first in Exodus and then in Deuteronomy.
It is also important to note that the first of the commandments isn’t
actually a commandment but rather a declaration. Accordingly, the list
is known in Hebrew as <span class="ital">Aseret ha-Dibrot</span>, the
Ten Statements, rather than <span class="ital">Aseret ha-Mitzvot</span>,
the Ten Commandments. / 186 <span class="bold1">Ten Commandments vs. the
Big Mac vs. The Brady Bunch:</span> Drawn from a report by Kelton
Research, “Motive Marketing: Ten Commandments Survey” (September 2007);
and Reuters Wire, “Americans Know Big Macs Better Than Ten
Commandments,” Reuters.com, October 12, 2007.

187 <span class="bold4"> CONSIDER ONE MORE STORY FROM THE BIBLE</span>:
This can be found in II Samuel: 12. We are indebted to Jonathan Rosen
for bringing to our attention how perfectly this story illustrated our
point. Some of the words used to tell it here are his, as we could not
improve upon them.

188 <span class="bold4"> ANTON CHEKHOV AND WHERE TO “CUT INTO” A
STORY</span>: For this insight, we are indebted to a long-ago writing
seminar taught by the great Richard Locke.

<span class="bold1">CHAPTER 9: THE UPSIDE OF QUITTING</span>

190 <span class="bold4"> CHURCHILL AND “NEVER GIVE IN”</span>:
Transcript provided by the Churchill Centre at www.winstonchurchill.org.

190 <span class="bold4"> “A QUITTER NEVER WINS, AND A WINNER NEVER
QUITS”</span>: In 1937, a self-help pundit named Napoleon Hill included
that phrase in his very popular book <span class="ital">Think and Grow
Rich</span>. Hill was inspired in part by the rags-to-riches
industrialist Andrew Carnegie. These days the phrase is often attributed
to Vince Lombardi, the legendarily tough football coach. For another
discussion of the idea presented in this chapter, with stories of
several different quitters, see Stephen J. Dubner, “The Upside of
Quitting,” Freakonomics Radio, September 30, 2011.

191 <span class="bold4"> THE CONCORDE FALLACY</span>: See Richard
Dawkins and H. Jane Brockmann, “Do Digger Wasps Commit the Concorde
Fallacy?,” <span class="ital">Animal Behavior</span> 28, 3 (1980);
Dawkins and T. R. Carlisle, “Parental Investment, Mate Desertion and a
Fallacy,” <span class="ital">Nature</span> 262, no. 131 (July 8, 1976).

191 <span class="bold4"> OPPORTUNITY COST IS HARDER:</span> For a lovely
and insightful essay that touches on the concept of opportunity cost,
see Frédéric Bastiat, “What Is Seen and What Is Not Seen,”
<span class="ital">Selected Essays on Political Economy,</span> first
published 1848; published 1995 by The Foundation for Economic Education,
Inc.

192 <span class="bold4"> MICHAEL BLOOMBERG AND FAILURE:</span> See James
Bennet, “The Bloomberg Way,” <span class="ital">The Atlantic,</span>
November 2012.

193 <span class="bold4"> INTELLECTUAL VENTURES AND THE SELF-STERILIZING
SURFACE:</span> Based on author interviews with Geoff Deane and other
Intellectual Ventures scientists. See also Katie Miller, “Q&A: Five Good
Questions,” Intellectual Ventures Lab blog, August 9, 2012; Nathan
Myhrvold, TEDMED 2010; and Nick Vu, “Self-Sterilizing Surfaces,”
Intellectual Ventures Lab blog, November 18, 2010. The primary patents
on the UV self-sterilizing surface are numbers 8,029,727, 8,029,740,
8,114,346, and 8,343,434.

197 <span class="bold4"> THE</span>
<span class="boldital">CHALLENGER</span>
<span class="bold4">EXPLOSION:</span> See Allan J. McDonald and James R.
Hansen, <span class="ital">Truth, Lies, and O-Rings: Inside the Space
Shuttle Challenger Disaster</span> (University Press of Florida, 2009);
also see Joe Atkinson, “Engineer Who Opposed Challenger Launch Offers
Personal Look at Tragedy,” <span class="ital">Researcher News
(NASA)</span>, October 5, 2012; and “Report of the Presidential
Commission on the Space Shuttle Challenger Accident,” June 6, 1986.

199 <span class="bold4"> THE “PREMORTEM”:</span> See Gary Klein,
“Performing a Project Premortem,” <span class="ital">Harvard Business
Review</span>, September 2007; Beth Veinott, Klein, and Sterling
Wiggins, “Evaluating the Effectiveness of the PreMortem Technique on
Plan Confidence,” Proceedings of the 7th International ISCRAM Conference
(May, 2010); Deborah J. Mitchell, J. Edward Russo, Nancy Pennington,
“Back to the Future: Temporal Perspective in the Explanation of Events,”
<span class="ital">Journal of Behavioral Decision Making</span> 2, no. 1
(1989). Thanks to Danny Kahneman for bringing the idea to our attention.

199 <span class="bold4"> CARSTEN WROSCH AND THE TOLL OF NOT
QUITTING:</span> See Carsten Wrosch, Gregory E. Miller, Michael F.
Scheier, Stephanie Brun de Pontet, “Giving Up on Unattainable Goals:
Benefits for Health?,” <span class="ital">Personality and Social
Psychology Bulletin</span> 33, no. 2 (February 2007). For a fuller
treatment, see Stephen J. Dubner, “The Upside of Quitting,” Freakonomics
Radio, June 30, 2011.

200 <span class="bold4"> FREAKONOMICS EXPERIMENTS:</span> The website
FreakonomicsExperiments.com is still active as of this writing and can
flip a coin for you, but the long-term tracking study is no longer
functional. For Steve Levitt’s fuller discussion on the topic, see
Stephen J. Dubner, “Would You Let a Coin Toss Decide Your Future?”
Freakonomics Radio, January 31, 2013. Perhaps the most heartbreaking
write-in question we received: “Should I leave my son with my wife until
she dies from cancer (approx. 8 months) so I can work in Africa to
support my family, or should I turn down the job in Africa and stay in
the U.S. to be near my son as I go broke?”

207 <span class="boldital"> COPS</span> <span class="bold4">AND THE
WRITERS’ STRIKE:</span> See Associated Press, “Strike May Test Reality
TV’s Staying Power,” November 27, 2007.

210 <span class="bold4"> WINSTON CHURCHILL AS “THE GREATEST OF ALL
BRITAIN’S WAR LEADERS”:</span> See John Keegan, “Winston Churchill,”
<span class="ital">Time</span>, June 24, 2001. Thanks to Jonathan Rosen
for conversations on this topic and to the author and Churchill scholar
Barry Singer for his continuing guidance on the topic.

<span class="ital">If you have a question we haven’t answered in these
notes, or have something to share, feel free to drop us a line at
ThinkLikeAFreak@Freakonomics.com.</span>

the Authors</span></a>

<div class="img-center">

<img src="images/00006.jpeg" class="calibre7" />

</div>

STEVEN D. LEVITT, a professor of economics at the University of Chicago,
was awarded the John Bates Clark medal, given to the most influential
American economist under the age of forty. He is also a founder of The
Greatest Good, which applies Freakonomics-style thinking to business and
philanthropy.

<div class="img-center">

<img src="images/00007.jpeg" class="calibre7" />

</div>

STEPHEN J. DUBNER is an award-winning author, journalist, and radio and
TV personality. He quit his first career—as an almost-rock-star—to
become a writer. He has worked for *The New York Times* and published
three non-Freakonomics books. He lives with his family in New York City.

Visit <a href="http://www.AuthorTracker.com" class="calibre2"><span
class="blue2">www.AuthorTracker.com</span></a>\
for exclusive information on your favorite HarperCollins authors.
